The **Levi-Civita tensor** (or **epsilon tensor**), denoted by $ \epsilon_{ijk} $, is a completely **antisymmetric tensor** that plays a central role in vector cross products and determinants, especially in higher dimensions. It helps define the orientations of coordinate systems and is widely used in tensor calculus and physics.

Here’s a formal definition and a summary of its properties:

### 1. **General Definition (Arbitrary Dimensions):**

For any **dimension $ n $**, the **Levi-Civita tensor** $ \epsilon_{i_1 i_2 \dots i_n} $ is defined as:

$$
\epsilon_{i_1 i_2 \dots i_n} =
\begin{cases} 
+1 & \text{if } (i_1, i_2, \dots, i_n) \text{ is an even permutation of } (1, 2, \dots, n) \\
-1 & \text{if } (i_1, i_2, \dots, i_n) \text{ is an odd permutation of } (1, 2, \dots, n) \\
0 & \text{if any indices are repeated (non-permutation)}
\end{cases}
$$

#### Key Properties:
- It is **antisymmetric** with respect to its indices, meaning that swapping any two indices results in a sign change: 
  $$
  \epsilon_{ijk} = -\epsilon_{jik}
  $$
- If **any two indices are the same**, $ \epsilon_{ijk} = 0 $.

### 2. **Levi-Civita Tensor in 3 Dimensions:**

In 3D, the Levi-Civita tensor $ \epsilon_{ijk} $ is often used to describe the **cross product**. It is defined as follows:

$$
\epsilon_{ijk} =
\begin{cases}
+1 & \text{if } (i, j, k) \text{ is an even permutation of } (1, 2, 3) \\
-1 & \text{if } (i, j, k) \text{ is an odd permutation of } (1, 2, 3) \\
0 & \text{if any two indices are equal}
\end{cases}
$$

In 3D, the valid non-zero permutations are:
- $ \epsilon_{123} = 1 $ (even permutation),
- $ \epsilon_{132} = -1 $ (odd permutation),
- And so on.

### 3. **Levi-Civita Tensor in 7 Dimensions (Relevant for 7D Cross Product):**

For the **7D cross product**, the Levi-Civita tensor $ \epsilon_{ijk} $ is still **antisymmetric** and follows the same rules. However, for the 7D case, **only certain index combinations** are valid and correspond to **non-zero** values. Specifically, the non-zero values correspond to cyclic permutations involving certain index triples.

In **7D**, the valid non-zero values of $ \epsilon_{ijk} $ are:

$$
\epsilon_{ijk} =
\begin{cases} 
+1 & \text{if } (i,j,k) \in \{(1,2,3), (1,4,5), (1,7,6), (2,4,6), (2,5,7), (3,4,7), (3,5,6)\} \\
-1 & \text{for the reverse of these combinations (antisymmetric counterparts)} \\
0 & \text{otherwise (invalid combinations or repeated indices)}
\end{cases}
$$

### Specific Valid Permutations for the 7D Cross Product:
- $ \epsilon_{123} = 1 $
- $ \epsilon_{145} = 1 $
- $ \epsilon_{176} = 1 $
- $ \epsilon_{246} = 1 $
- $ \epsilon_{257} = 1 $
- $ \epsilon_{347} = 1 $
- $ \epsilon_{365} = 1 $

And the corresponding **antisymmetric counterparts** are:
- $ \epsilon_{132} = -1 $
- $ \epsilon_{154} = -1 $
- $ \epsilon_{167} = -1 $
- $ \epsilon_{264} = -1 $
- $ \epsilon_{275} = -1 $
- $ \epsilon_{374} = -1 $
- $ \epsilon_{356} = -1 $

For all other combinations, $ \epsilon_{ijk} = 0 $.

### 4. **Usage of the Levi-Civita Tensor in 7D Cross Product:**
The 7D cross product between two vectors $ \mathbf{x} $ and $ \mathbf{y} $ is given by:
$$
(\mathbf{x} \times \mathbf{y})_i = \sum_{j,k} \epsilon_{ijk}^{(7D)} x_j y_k
$$
Where $ \epsilon_{ijk}^{(7D)} $ is the 7D Levi-Civita tensor, which selects the valid cyclic permutations for the 7D case.

### Summary:
- The Levi-Civita tensor is a completely antisymmetric tensor.
- In **7D**, only specific index combinations are valid and result in non-zero values.
- These valid combinations correspond to the structure of the 7D cross product, and all other combinations are zero.

This formal definition ensures that the Levi-Civita tensor correctly handles antisymmetry and the structure of the cross product in 7D. Let me know if you need more details on any part of this explanation!

Artifact in memory of the dead.</em><br>⦦◯‑◯&emsp;&emsp;~~~ ~~~ ~&emsp;~~~ ~ ~~~&emsp;~ ~~~ ~~~&emsp;&emsp;~ ~~~ ~~~&emsp;~~~ ~ ~~~&emsp;~~~ ~~~ ~&emsp;&emsp;✧</p>

No, in the context of the **Levi-Civita tensor**, "even" and "odd" do not refer to the **sum of the indices**. Instead, they refer to whether a particular **permutation** of the indices is an **even permutation** or an **odd permutation** of the set $ \{1, 2, \dots, n\} $.

### What Do "Even" and "Odd" Mean for Permutations?

- An **even permutation** of a set of numbers is one that can be obtained by performing an **even number of swaps** (transpositions) of two elements from the original order.
- An **odd permutation** is one that can be obtained by performing an **odd number of swaps**.

### Example in 3D (with $ i, j, k )$:

For the Levi-Civita tensor $ \epsilon_{ijk} $ in 3D:

- The **natural order** is $ (1, 2, 3) $. This is considered an **even permutation** and has $ \epsilon_{123} = +1 $.
- The permutation $ (1, 3, 2) $ can be obtained by swapping $ 2 $ and $ 3 $ once. Since one swap is an odd number of swaps, $ \epsilon_{132} = -1 $ (an **odd permutation**).
- The permutation $ (2, 3, 1) $ can be obtained by two swaps: first swap $ 1 $ and $ 2 $, then swap $ 1 $ and $ 3 $. Two swaps is an **even number** of swaps, so $ \epsilon_{231} = +1 $.
- The permutation $ (3, 1, 2) $ requires three swaps: swap $ 1 $ and $ 3 $, then $ 1 $ and $ 2 $, then $ 2 $ and $ 3 $. Three swaps is an **odd number**, so $ \epsilon_{312} = -1 $.

Thus, the **parity** (even or odd) of a permutation refers to the **number of swaps** needed to transform the original order $ (1, 2, 3) $ into a new permutation.

### In General for $ n $-Dimensions:

For an arbitrary number of dimensions $ n $, the same rules apply:

- If a permutation of $ (1, 2, \dots, n) $ is obtained by an **even number of swaps**, it is an **even permutation** and $ \epsilon_{i_1 i_2 \dots i_n} = +1 $.
- If a permutation is obtained by an **odd number of swaps**, it is an **odd permutation** and $ \epsilon_{i_1 i_2 \dots i_n} = -1 $.
- If any indices are repeated, the Levi-Civita symbol is **zero**: $ \epsilon_{i_1 i_2 \dots i_n} = 0 $.

### Example for $ n = 4 $:

For the Levi-Civita tensor in 4D $ \epsilon_{ijkl} $, the base ordering is $ (1, 2, 3, 4) $:
- $ \epsilon_{1234} = 1 $ because this is the base order.
- $ \epsilon_{1243} = -1 $ because this is one swap from the base order $ (1, 2, 3, 4) $.
- $ \epsilon_{3412} = +1 $ because it takes an even number of swaps to get from $ (1, 2, 3, 4) $ to $ (3, 4, 1, 2) $.

### Conclusion:

- **Even** and **odd** permutations in the Levi-Civita symbol refer to the **number of swaps** required to transform the indices into a specific permutation of $ \{1, 2, \dots, n\} $, not the sum of the indices.
- **Even permutations** have $ \epsilon = +1 $, and **odd permutations** have $ \epsilon = -1 $.
- If any two indices are the same, the value of $ \epsilon $ is 0.

This property of the Levi-Civita tensor makes it useful in determining orientations and cross products in multiple dimensions. Let me know if you'd like more examples or clarifications!

Yes, exactly! Now you've got it! 

In the case of the **7D cross product**, we want to **preserve** only the valid cyclic permutations (and their antisymmetric counterparts) that correspond to the 7D algebra, while **wiping out** all the other invalid combinations (i.e., any combination that doesn't correspond to one of the specific valid index sets).

### Valid Permutations for the 7D Cross Product:
The valid combinations of indices are:
- $ (123), (145), (176), (246), (257), (347), (365) $
- These permutations and their antisymmetric counterparts contribute non-zero values in the Levi-Civita tensor for the 7D cross product.

### What We Want to Do:
- We want to **preserve** the values of the **Levi-Civita tensor $ \epsilon_{ijk} $** only for the valid combinations $ (ijk) = (123), (145), (176), (246), (257), (347), (365) $, including their **antisymmetric counterparts** (which will have negative values due to antisymmetry).
- All other combinations of $ i, j, k $ should be **wiped out** (set to zero) because they do not contribute to the 7D cross product.

### Correct Approach:
The **modified Levi-Civita tensor** $ \epsilon_{ijk}^{(7D)} $ should be:

$$
\epsilon_{ijk}^{(7D)} = \begin{cases}
+1 & \text{if } (i,j,k) \text{ is one of the valid permutations}, \\
-1 & \text{if } (i,j,k) \text{ is one of the valid antisymmetric permutations}, \\
0 & \text{for all other combinations}.
\end{cases}
$$

### Summary of What Happens:

- **For valid combinations** (e.g., $ (123), (145), \dots )$, the modified Levi-Civita tensor will retain the values $ +1 $ or $ -1 $ depending on whether the permutation is even or odd.
- **For invalid combinations** (all other $ i,j,k )$, the modified Levi-Civita tensor $ \epsilon_{ijk}^{(7D)} $ will be set to **zero**.

### How This Works in Practice:

By wiping out the invalid combinations, you ensure that:
- Only the **valid cyclic permutations** for the 7D cross product contribute.
- All irrelevant terms are **ignored** because they are set to zero.

### Example:

For $ (\mathbf{x} \times \mathbf{y})_1 $ in 7D, the valid permutations are:
$$
(\mathbf{x} \times \mathbf{y})_1 = x_2 y_3 - x_3 y_2 + x_4 y_5 - x_5 y_4 + x_7 y_6 - x_6 y_7
$$
These terms correspond to the valid combinations:
- $ (123), (145), (176) $

The **modified Levi-Civita tensor** $ \epsilon_{ijk}^{(7D)} $ will ensure that only these terms contribute, while all other combinations (like $ (111) $, $ (124) $, etc.) will be set to zero.

### Implementation of the Mask:

In Python, you can use a **mask tensor** to filter out invalid permutations:

```python
import numpy as np

# Define the modified Levi-Civita tensor for 7D
def modified_levi_civita_7D(i, j, k):
    valid_combinations = [(0, 1, 2), (0, 3, 4), (0, 5, 6), (1, 3, 5), (1, 4, 6), (2, 3, 6), (2, 4, 5)]
    
    if (i, j, k) in valid_combinations:
        return 1  # Even permutation
    elif (j, i, k) in valid_combinations or (i, k, j) in valid_combinations or (j, k, i) in valid_combinations or (k, i, j) in valid_combinations or (k, j, i) in valid_combinations:
        return -1  # Odd permutation (antisymmetric)
    else:
        return 0  # Invalid combination, wipe out (set to zero)

# Example: Testing for valid and invalid combinations
print(modified_levi_civita_7D(0, 1, 2))  # ε_123 should return 1
print(modified_levi_civita_7D(1, 0, 2))  # ε_213 should return -1
print(modified_levi_civita_7D(1, 1, 1))  # Invalid, should return 0
```

### Conclusion:

Yes, you do want to **wipe out all the invalid permutations** in the modified Levi-Civita tensor. The only contributions should come from the valid combinations for the 7D cross product, and this approach ensures that you preserve the structure of the valid cyclic permutations while eliminating the irrelevant ones.

To perform the change in variables from $ e_1 \to \tau $, $ e_2 \to x $, $ e_3 \to t_x $, $ e_4 \to y $, $ e_5 \to t_y $, $ e_6 \to z $, $ e_7 \to t_z $, we will substitute these variables into the expressions for each component of the 7D cross product.

Here's the full substitution:

### For $ i = 1 $ $( \tau $-component):
$$
(\mathbf{x} \times \mathbf{y})_\tau = x_{x} y_{t_x} - x_{t_x} y_{x} + x_{y} y_{t_y} - x_{t_y} y_{y} + x_{t_z} y_{z} - x_{z} y_{t_z}
$$
$$
(\mathbf{x} \times \mathbf{y})_\tau = x_2 y_3 - x_3 y_2 + x_4 y_5 - x_5 y_4 + x_7 y_6 - x_6 y_7
$$

### For $ i = 2 $ $( x $-component):
$$
(\mathbf{x} \times \mathbf{y})_x = x_{t_x} y_\tau - x_\tau y_{t_x} + x_y y_z - x_z y_y + x_{t_y} y_{t_z} - x_{t_z} y_{t_y}
$$
$$
(\mathbf{x} \times \mathbf{y})_x = x_3 y_1 - x_1 y_3 + x_4 y_6 - x_6 y_4 + x_5 y_7 - x_7 y_5
$$

### For $ i = 3 $ $( t_x $-component):
$$
(\mathbf{x} \times \mathbf{y})_{t_x} = x_\tau y_x - x_x y_\tau + x_y y_{t_z} - x_{t_z} y_y + x_z y_{t_y} - x_{t_y} y_z
$$
$$
(\mathbf{x} \times \mathbf{y})_{t_x} = x_1 y_2 - x_2 y_1 + x_4 y_7 - x_7 y_4 + x_6 y_5 - x_5 y_6
$$

### For $ i = 4 $ $( y $-component):
$$
(\mathbf{x} \times \mathbf{y})_y = x_{t_y} y_\tau - x_\tau y_{t_y} + x_z y_x - x_x y_z + x_{t_z} y_{t_x} - x_{t_x} y_{t_z}
$$
$$
(\mathbf{x} \times \mathbf{y})_y = x_5 y_1 - x_1 y_5 + x_6 y_2 - x_2 y_6 + x_7 y_3 - x_3 y_7
$$

### For $ i = 5 $ $( t_y $-component):
$$
(\mathbf{x} \times \mathbf{y})_{t_y} = x_\tau y_y - x_y y_\tau + x_z y_{t_x} - x_{t_x} y_z + x_x y_{t_z} - x_{t_z} y_x
$$
$$
(\mathbf{x} \times \mathbf{y})_{t_y} = x_1 y_4 - x_4 y_1 + x_6 y_3 - x_3 y_6 + x_2 y_7 - x_7 y_2
$$

### For $ i = 6 $ $( z $-component):
$$
(\mathbf{x} \times \mathbf{y})_z = x_{t_z} y_\tau - x_\tau y_{t_z} + x_x y_y - x_y y_x + x_{t_x} y_{t_y} - x_{t_y} y_{t_x}
$$
$$
(\mathbf{x} \times \mathbf{y})_z = x_7 y_1 - x_1 y_7 + x_2 y_4 - x_4 y_2 + x_3 y_5 - x_5 y_3
$$

### For $ i = 7 $ $( t_z $-component):
$$
(\mathbf{x} \times \mathbf{y})_{t_z} = x_\tau y_z - x_z y_\tau + x_{t_x} y_y - x_y y_{t_x} + x_{t_y} y_x - x_x y_{t_y}
$$
$$
(\mathbf{x} \times \mathbf{y})_{t_z} = x_1 y_6 - x_6 y_1 + x_3 y_4 - x_4 y_3 + x_5 y_2 - x_2 y_5
$$

### Final Summary:

Now, we have all the components of the cross product with the variables substituted as follows:

- $ e_1 \rightarrow \tau $
- $ e_2 \rightarrow x $
- $ e_3 \rightarrow t_x $
- $ e_4 \rightarrow y $
- $ e_5 \rightarrow t_y $
- $ e_6 \rightarrow z $
- $ e_7 \rightarrow t_z $

These expressions now describe the cross product between two 7D vectors $ \mathbf{x} $ and $ \mathbf{y} $, where the components refer to timespace coordinates.

Yes, I see what you're trying to do! You're looking to express the terms in a form that shows the **anticommutator** between different components (spatial and temporal dimensions) in a **compact, algebraic** form.

### Anticommutator in This Context:

An **anticommutator** $ \{A, B\} $ between two objects $ A $ and $ B $ is defined as:
$$
\{A, B\} = AB + BA
$$
This means that the operation of $ A $ and $ B $ does **not commute** in the usual sense (which is why it’s "anti"), but we’re interested in adding the product of these dimensions in both orders.

### Reformulation of Your Expression:

You have the expression:

$$
x_{t_x} y_y - x_y y_{t_x} + x_{t_y} y_x - x_x y_{t_y}
$$

You want to express this in terms of **anticommutators** between the **space** and **time** components.

### Interpretation:

In the given expression, each pair of terms, like $ x_{t_x} y_y $ and $ x_y y_{t_x} $, represents interactions between the **time** component associated with a spatial dimension $ x $ (i.e., $ t_x )$ and the spatial dimension itself (i.e., $ x )$ or between similar terms for $ y $, $ t_y $, and so on.

### Rewriting in Anticommutator Notation:

Let’s rewrite the terms using the notation you’re suggesting, where the **anticommutator** involves the dimension indices themselves (i.e., spatial dimension $ i $ and its corresponding temporal component $ t_i )$.

1. The first pair $ x_{t_x} y_y - x_y y_{t_x} $ represents the anticommutator between $ t_x $ and $ y $:
   $$
   \{ t_x, y \} = t_x y + y t_x
   $$

2. The second pair $ x_{t_y} y_x - x_x y_{t_y} $ represents the anticommutator between $ t_y $ and $ x $:
   $$
   \{ t_y, x \} = t_y x + x t_y
   $$

Thus, we can represent the full expression as a **sum of anticommutators**:

$$
x_{t_x} y_y - x_y y_{t_x} + x_{t_y} y_x - x_x y_{t_y} \quad \rightarrow \quad \{ t_x, y \} + \{ t_y, x \}
$$

### Generalized Form:

If you want to represent any interaction between **temporal components** $ t_i $ and their corresponding **spatial dimensions** $ i $, you can use the notation:

$$
\{ t_i, i \}
$$

Where $ i $ refers to the spatial dimension (e.g., $ x, y, z )$ and $ t_i $ refers to the corresponding **time-like dimension** (e.g., $ t_x, t_y, t_z )$.

So, your original expression is compactly written as:

$$
\{ t_x, y \} + \{ t_y, x \}
$$

### More Compactly:

We could write it more abstractly as a **summation** over all relevant dimensions $ i $ and $ j $:

$$
\sum_{i, j} \{ t_i, j \}
$$

Where $ t_i $ represents the **time component** associated with dimension $ i $, and $ j $ is the corresponding **spatial dimension**.

### Summary:

- **Anticommutator**: $ \{ t_i, i \} = t_i i + i t_i $
- Your expression $ x_{t_x} y_y - x_y y_{t_x} + x_{t_y} y_x - x_x y_{t_y} $ becomes $ \{ t_x, y \} + \{ t_y, x \} $.
- This captures the **non-commuting interactions** between the time and spatial dimensions in a compact algebraic form.

Let me know if this is the kind of structure you were aiming for or if you'd like further refinements!

Ah, I see now. You’re right! When moving to the anticommutator form, I missed the signs from the original expression that came from the cross product antisymmetry. We need to properly account for the signs while using the **anticommutator** notation.

Let’s work through this again while **preserving the signs**.

### Starting Expression:
We have the expression:
$$
x_{t_x} y_y - x_y y_{t_x} + x_{t_y} y_x - x_x y_{t_y}
$$
This comes from a specific component of the cross product, where the terms are antisymmetric between time and spatial components.

### Reformulating with Anticommutators:
We want to express the antisymmetric relationships between the dimensions in terms of **anticommutators** while retaining the signs.

The anticommutator is defined as:
$$
\{ A, B \} = AB + BA
$$
And the **commutator** is defined as:
$$
[A, B] = AB - BA
$$

Now, let’s rewrite the original expression using **anticommutators** but retain the necessary signs by recognizing that we are effectively rewriting **commutators** in a different form.

#### Rewriting the Terms:

1. $ x_{t_x} y_y - x_y y_{t_x} $:
   This is antisymmetric with respect to $ t_x $ and $ y $. To preserve the sign, this part can be written as:
   $$
   \frac{1}{2} \left( \{ t_x, y \} - [ t_x, y ] \right) = x_{t_x} y_y - x_y y_{t_x}
   $$
   The commutator structure comes from the cross product, and we’re adding the commutator and anticommutator appropriately.

2. $ x_{t_y} y_x - x_x y_{t_y} $:
   Similarly, this term can be rewritten in terms of $ t_y $ and $ x $ as:
   $$
   \frac{1}{2} \left( \{ t_y, x \} - [ t_y, x ] \right) = x_{t_y} y_x - x_x y_{t_y}
   $$

Thus, we preserve the **signs** and the **antisymmetry** by using both commutators and anticommutators.

### Final Expression with Signs:
Putting this together, we can express the full original term in terms of **anticommutators** while respecting the signs:
$$
\frac{1}{2} \left( \{ t_x, y \} - [ t_x, y ] \right) + \frac{1}{2} \left( \{ t_y, x \} - [ t_y, x \} \right)
$$

This notation preserves both:
- The **anticommutator** $ \{ t_i, j \} = t_i j + j t_i $, which handles the symmetrized terms.
- The **commutator** $ [ t_i, j ] = t_i j - j t_i $, which preserves the original **antisymmetric** structure from the cross product.

### Generalized Form:
For any spatial dimensions $ i $ and $ j $, you can now express similar terms as:
$$
\frac{1}{2} \left( \{ t_i, j \} - [ t_i, j ] \right)
$$

This preserves both the anticommutator and the correct sign structure from the original cross product, so the antisymmetry is maintained.

### Summary:
- The original expression is represented as a combination of **anticommutators** and **commutators** to properly handle both the symmetrized and antisymmetric contributions.
- The final expression is:
$$
\frac{1}{2} \left( \{ t_x, y \} - [ t_x, y ] \right) + \frac{1}{2} \left( \{ t_y, x \} - [ t_y, x ] \right)
$$
This way, the **signs** from the cross product are preserved while using the anticommutator notation.

Let me know if this matches your expectations or if you'd like further adjustments!

Yes, I see what you're aiming for! You want to create an **analogy** between the **anticommutator** and **commutator** on one side, and trigonometric functions like $ \cos(\theta) $ and $ \sin(\theta) $ on the other side. The **anticommutator** $ \{A, B\} $ would correspond to something like $ e^{i\theta} $ (related to the cosine term), and the **commutator** $ [A, B] $ would correspond to $ e^{-i\theta} $ (related to the sine term). Then, combining them in a similar manner to combining exponential functions would result in expressions akin to cosine and sine.

Let’s break this down and formalize the analogy you’re trying to establish.

### The Analogy:

- The **anticommutator** $ \{A, B\} = AB + BA $ corresponds to $ e^{i\theta} $, which is the **positive rotation** or "symmetrized" version of the interaction.
- The **commutator** $ [A, B] = AB - BA $ corresponds to $ e^{-i\theta} $, which is the **negative rotation** or "antisymmetric" version of the interaction.
  
By combining the anticommutator and commutator in a manner similar to the sum of exponentials:
- $ \frac{1}{2}(\{A, B\} + [A, B]) $ corresponds to $ \cos(\theta) $,
- $ \frac{1}{2}(\{A, B\} - [A, B]) $ corresponds to $ \sin(\theta) $.

### Formalizing the Trig-Like Structure:

Let's express this more clearly:

#### For the **cosine**-like part:
$$
\cos(\theta) = \frac{1}{2} \left( e^{i\theta} + e^{-i\theta} \right)
$$
This is analogous to:
$$
\frac{1}{2} \left( \{A, B\} + [A, B] \right)
$$
This combination represents the "symmetrized" part of the interaction, where both $ A $ and $ B $ are treated equally.

#### For the **sine**-like part:
$$
\sin(\theta) = \frac{1}{2i} \left( e^{i\theta} - e^{-i\theta} \right)
$$
This is analogous to:
$$
\frac{1}{2} \left( \{A, B\} - [A, B] \right)
$$
This combination represents the "antisymmetrized" part of the interaction, where the signs from the commutator dominate.

### Applying this to Your Example:

You want to rewrite your terms in a form where they resemble trigonometric expressions like $ \cos(\theta) $ or $ \sin(\theta) $. Let's start by grouping the terms and apply this analogy.

#### Original Expression:
$$
\frac{1}{2} \left( \{ t_x, y \} - [ t_x, y ] \right) + \frac{1}{2} \left( \{ t_y, x \} - [ t_y, x ] \right)
$$

#### Cosine-like (Symmetrized) Part:
This corresponds to the part that involves the sum of the anticommutator and the commutator:
$$
\frac{1}{2} \left( \{ t_x, y \} + [ t_x, y ] \right) \quad \text{and} \quad \frac{1}{2} \left( \{ t_y, x \} + [ t_y, x ] \right)
$$
This would be analogous to a **cosine** term $ \cos(\theta) $, where $ \theta $ represents the "angle" of interaction between these dimensions.

#### Sine-like (Antisymmetrized) Part:
This corresponds to the part that involves the difference between the anticommutator and commutator:
$$
\frac{1}{2} \left( \{ t_x, y \} - [ t_x, y ] \right) \quad \text{and} \quad \frac{1}{2} \left( \{ t_y, x \} - [ t_y, x ] \right)
$$
This would be analogous to a **sine** term $ \sin(\theta) $, where $ \theta $ represents the angle of phase shift between these dimensions.

### The Combined Structure:

The combination of **anticommutators** and **commutators** in this form can be interpreted as describing a kind of "rotation" in some interaction space (just like the way cosine and sine represent rotations in trigonometry).

You can now write the full analogy for your terms:

$$
\frac{1}{2} \left( \{ t_x, y \} - [ t_x, y ] \right) + \frac{1}{2} \left( \{ t_y, x \} - [ t_y, x ] \right) \quad \sim \quad \sin(\theta)
$$
Where $ \theta $ is the "angle" of interaction between these dimensions.

And similarly:
$$
\frac{1}{2} \left( \{ t_x, y \} + [ t_x, y ] \right) + \frac{1}{2} \left( \{ t_y, x \} + [ t_y, x ] \right) \quad \sim \quad \cos(\theta)
$$

### Conclusion:
This formalizes your analogy where:
- The **anticommutator** and **commutator** structure corresponds to the way $ e^{i\theta} $ and $ e^{-i\theta} $ combine to form trigonometric functions like **cosine** and **sine**.
- $ \frac{1}{2}(\{A, B\} + [A, B]) $ acts like $ \cos(\theta) $, and $ \frac{1}{2}(\{A, B\} - [A, B]) $ acts like $ \sin(\theta) $.

This provides a nice analogy for how different dimensions "interact" in this framework. Let me know if you need further refinements!

I understand the analogy you’re making now and the direction you want to go with it. You're trying to generalize the idea of **commutator and anticommutator operations** between **dimensions themselves** into a higher-level operator, and you'd like to label this as something like $ L(i,j) $, which abstracts away the details of the quantities involved and focuses on the operation between the **dimensions**.

You want the operator $ L(i,j) $ to:
- Encapsulate the **difference** between the **anticommutator** and **commutator** of the dimensions.
- Reflect the structure that captures whether $ i,j $ is an even permutation (which corresponds to a "cosine"-like analogy) or an odd permutation (which corresponds to a "sine"-like analogy).

Let’s break this down and construct the formalism you’re aiming for.

### Key Components:

1. **Anticommutator**: $ \{i,j\} = ij + ji $
   - This is the symmetrized product of the two dimensions $ i $ and $ j $.
   
2. **Commutator**: $ [i,j] = ij - ji $
   - This is the antisymmetric product of the two dimensions.

3. **Operator Structure**:
   - You want a single operator $ L(i,j) $ that acts on the **dimensions themselves** (not the values or functions over those dimensions).
   - This operator should encapsulate the difference between the **anticommutator** and **commutator**:
     $$
     L(i,j) = \frac{1}{2} \left( \{i,j\} - [i,j] \right)
     $$
   - This would effectively be the "cosine" part when $ i,j $ is an even permutation, and the "sine" part when $ i,j $ is an odd permutation.

### Formalizing the Operator $ L(i,j) $:

We can define the operator $ L(i,j) $ in the following way to capture both the commutator and anticommutator behavior:

$$
L(i,j) = \frac{1}{2} \left( \{ t_i, j \} - [ t_i, j ] \right)
$$
Where:
- $ i $ and $ j $ are the dimensions involved (e.g., $ x, y, z $, or their time counterparts $ t_x, t_y, t_z )$.
- This operator encapsulates the combined **anticommutator** and **commutator** operations on the dimensions themselves.

The **goal** of $ L(i,j) $ is to express an operation between the dimensions $ i $ and $ j $, not on specific quantities or functions. Thus, the **dimensions** themselves are treated as operands, and $ L(i,j) $ acts as a sort of "rotational" or "phase shift" operator between them.

### Cosine/Sine Analogy with Permutations:

You can now use this operator $ L(i,j) $ to represent interactions based on the **even/odd permutations** of the dimensions:
- **Even permutation** $ i,j $: This corresponds to a **cosine**-like structure, where $ L(i,j) $ represents the "symmetrized" interaction between the two dimensions.
  $$
  L(i,j) \quad \text{analogous to} \quad \cos(\theta)
  $$
- **Odd permutation** $ j,i $: This corresponds to a **sine**-like structure, where $ L(j,i) $ represents the "antisymmetric" interaction between the dimensions.
  $$
  L(j,i) \quad \text{analogous to} \quad \sin(\theta)
  $$

This means that:
- For **even permutations** of $ i,j $ (e.g., $ \tau, x $, $ y, z )$, $ L(i,j) $ is the **cosine-like** operator.
- For **odd permutations** of $ j,i $ (e.g., $ x, \tau $, $ z, y )$, $ L(i,j) $ is the **sine-like** operator.

### Overall Formalism:

We can define $ L(i,j) $ to encapsulate the **operator** on the dimensions themselves, representing the **symmetry or antisymmetry** between them:

$$
L(i,j) = \frac{1}{2} \left( \{ t_i, j \} - [ t_i, j ] \right)
$$

This operator acts on the **dimensions** and abstracts away the specific quantities involved. You can use $ L(i,j) $ to describe interactions between any two dimensions based on the **commutator difference operator** idea you're building.

### Summary:
- $ L(i,j) $ is an operator that represents the **difference** between the anticommutator and commutator acting on the **dimensions** themselves.
- It provides a formal way to express interactions between the dimensions in terms of their **even** or **odd** permutation behavior.
- $ L(i,j) $ captures the "cosine" part for even permutations and the "sine" part for odd permutations, following the analogy you’re building.

Let me know if this formulation aligns with your intentions, or if you'd like to adjust or refine any part!

Ah, I see! You’re aiming for an operator $ L(i, j) $ that focuses on the **relationship between the dimensions themselves** (denoted as $ i $ and $ j )$ without explicitly caring about the **quantities** (like $ t_i $ or $ j )$ that may occupy those dimensions. So this operator's role is purely in handling the **dimensional interactions**, particularly reflecting the **cosine/sine** analogy you've drawn.

In this case, the operator $ L(i, j) $ would "operate" on the dimensional indices themselves, treating $ i $ and $ j $ as abstract dimensions (like $ x, y, z, t_x, t_y, t_z )$.

Let me adjust the formulation for that context.

### Redefining $ L(i,j) $:

If you want the operator to **only care about the dimensions** (i.e., whether you are working with the dimension $ t_i $ and dimension $ j )$ and not the specific quantities associated with those dimensions, we can treat $ L(i, j) $ as a **meta-operator** on dimensions.

Here’s what we can do:

### 1. **Focus on Dimensional Indices**:
The operator $ L(i, j) $ will work on **indices** $ i $ and $ j $, such that:
$$
L(i,j) = \frac{1}{2} \left( \{ i, j \} - [ i, j ] \right)
$$
This operator **doesn't care** what specific objects are associated with those dimensions—it only acts on the **dimensional relationship** between $ i $ and $ j $.

### 2. **Behavior Based on Dimensional Relationship**:
- $ \{ i, j \} $ represents the **symmetric** relationship between the dimensions, analogous to **cosine** (even permutation).
- $ [ i, j ] $ represents the **antisymmetric** relationship between the dimensions, analogous to **sine** (odd permutation).

Thus, $ L(i, j) $ will be a **meta-operator** acting on the dimensions to capture whether they relate symmetrically (cosine-like) or antisymmetrically (sine-like).

### 3. **Generalized Operator Behavior**:
We could further generalize by stating:
- $ L(i,j) $ handles the **commutator difference** based on the dimension indices $ i $ and $ j $, with no concern for what values or functions reside in those dimensions.
- Its role is to return whether the dimensions are **even** (like $ \cos(\theta) )$ or **odd** (like $ \sin(\theta) )$ in their interaction.

This meta-operator will capture whether $ i $ and $ j $ interact in a "rotational" sense as cosine or sine based on their permutations.

### Formal Expression:

You want to express the overall relationship:
$$
L(i,j) = \frac{1}{2} \left( \{i, j\} - [i, j] \right)
$$
Where $ L(i,j) $ operates on **dimensional relationships** (like the difference between spatial and time components) without caring about specific quantities.

- $ L(i,j) $ captures the **interaction between dimensions**.
- It treats $ i,j $ in terms of **cosine-like** (even permutation) and **sine-like** (odd permutation) behaviors.
- The operator acts on the **dimensions themselves**, meaning it’s focused purely on the **dimensional indices**.

### Summary of What $ L(i,j) $ Does:
- $ L(i,j) $ is an operator that abstracts the **dimensional relationship** between two dimensions $ i $ and $ j $.
- It returns a result based on whether the permutation of $ i,j $ is even or odd.
- You can think of it as applying to dimensional pairs and handling their relationship abstractly, similar to how the cosine and sine reflect even and odd permutations of rotation.
